========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’m here because I’ve felt… down, for a really long time. Not like one bad day or a rough week, but more like I’m just carrying around this flat, heavy feeling most days. It’s hard to explain because things can be going fine on the outside — like work might be okay, or someone might be nice to me, or nothing specifically bad is happening — but I still feel kind of empty underneath it.

It’s like my mood just doesn’t really lift the way I think it should. I keep going through my day, doing what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this constant low feeling in the background. After a while it started to feel less like “I’m having a bad mood” and more like this is just how I am now, and that’s what made me think I should probably talk to someone.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them in the same way or if I’m just doing them because they’re familiar, if that makes sense.

So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.

Like if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.

So… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. It’s not very clear-cut for me.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I can still recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, and sometimes I do get moments where I think, okay, this is nice, or this is better than just sitting around. But it’s not like this strong feeling of pleasure, if that makes sense.

A lot of the time it feels kind of muted. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.

So I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.

That’s a big part of why I came in. It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.

I kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.

But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I’ve felt… down, for a really long time. Not like one bad day or a rough week, but more like I’m just carrying around this flat, heavy feeling most days. It’s hard to explain because things can be going fine on the outside — like work might be okay, or someone might be nice to me, or nothing specifically bad is happening — but I still feel kind of empty underneath it.\n\nIt’s like my mood just doesn’t really lift the way I think it should. I keep going through my day, doing what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this constant low feeling in the background. After a while it started to feel less like “I’m having a bad mood” and more like this is just how I am now, and that’s what made me think I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them in the same way or if I’m just doing them because they’re familiar, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “Oh, I suddenly stopped liking that.” It’s more blurry than that. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of something that should be relaxing or fun, and I’ll catch myself kind of checking whether I’m having a good time, which probably makes it harder to actually enjoy it. So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.\n\nLike if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one I do notice. It’s not like I’m falling asleep at my desk all the time, exactly, but by the afternoon I feel really worn down. Like my body is technically there, but everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort.

At work, even something that should be small—like fixing an error in a spreadsheet, or finishing expense paperwork—can feel like I’m trying to push through mud with my brain. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.

And it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.

So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.\n\nLike if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. It’s not very clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure from activities or things that are typically enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I’ve felt… down, for a really long time. Not like one bad day or a rough week, but more like I’m just carrying around this flat, heavy feeling most days. It’s hard to explain because things can be going fine on the outside — like work might be okay, or someone might be nice to me, or nothing specifically bad is happening — but I still feel kind of empty underneath it.\n\nIt’s like my mood just doesn’t really lift the way I think it should. I keep going through my day, doing what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this constant low feeling in the background. After a while it started to feel less like “I’m having a bad mood” and more like this is just how I am now, and that’s what made me think I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them in the same way or if I’m just doing them because they’re familiar, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “Oh, I suddenly stopped liking that.” It’s more blurry than that. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of something that should be relaxing or fun, and I’ll catch myself kind of checking whether I’m having a good time, which probably makes it harder to actually enjoy it. So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.\n\nLike if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. It’s not very clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure from activities or things that are typically enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I can still recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, and sometimes I do get moments where I think, okay, this is nice, or this is better than just sitting around. But it’s not like this strong feeling of pleasure, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it feels kind of muted. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve had a period where I was, like, completely unable to function day to day. I still go to work, I still get through the basics, I pay bills, I eat, I show up most of the time. So if you mean totally unable to carry out daily activities, then no, not in that extreme way.

But it has definitely affected how well I’m functioning. My performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of hard to read because that didn’t used to be something people said about me. It’s like I start things or I know what needs to happen, but then I don’t carry it all the way through consistently.

And outside of work, I’ve been dropping things I used to stay on top of. Soccer commitments, volunteering stuff, even planning for an MBA, which I used to be pretty organized about. I wouldn’t say I can’t do anything, but I’m not keeping up with my life the way I used to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I’ve felt… down, for a really long time. Not like one bad day or a rough week, but more like I’m just carrying around this flat, heavy feeling most days. It’s hard to explain because things can be going fine on the outside — like work might be okay, or someone might be nice to me, or nothing specifically bad is happening — but I still feel kind of empty underneath it.\n\nIt’s like my mood just doesn’t really lift the way I think it should. I keep going through my day, doing what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this constant low feeling in the background. After a while it started to feel less like “I’m having a bad mood” and more like this is just how I am now, and that’s what made me think I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them in the same way or if I’m just doing them because they’re familiar, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “Oh, I suddenly stopped liking that.” It’s more blurry than that. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of something that should be relaxing or fun, and I’ll catch myself kind of checking whether I’m having a good time, which probably makes it harder to actually enjoy it. So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.\n\nLike if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. It’s not very clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure from activities or things that are typically enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I can still recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, and sometimes I do get moments where I think, okay, this is nice, or this is better than just sitting around. But it’s not like this strong feeling of pleasure, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it feels kind of muted. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had rough periods before, like stressful semesters or times where I felt down about something, but I don’t know if I’d put them in the same category as this.

About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some obvious, specific sleep problem that I can point to and say, “yes, this is definitely it.”\n\nI mean, I have normal nights where I sleep fine, and then nights where I’m kind of restless or I wake up and feel like I didn’t get enough, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just normal life stuff. I haven’t been tracking it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say with confidence.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a major sleep issue, at least not one that feels clear or consistent enough for me to describe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I do notice. It’s not like I’m falling asleep at my desk all the time, exactly, but by the afternoon I feel really worn down. Like my body is technically there, but everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort.\n\nAt work, even something that should be small—like fixing an error in a spreadsheet, or finishing expense paperwork—can feel like I’m trying to push through mud with my brain. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. No… not like this. I’ve been upset before, or stressed, or had times where I felt low for a bit, but this is different.

But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some obvious, specific sleep problem that I can point to and say, “yes, this is definitely it.”\n\nI mean, I have normal nights where I sleep fine, and then nights where I’m kind of restless or I wake up and feel like I didn’t get enough, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just normal life stuff. I haven’t been tracking it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say with confidence.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a major sleep issue, at least not one that feels clear or consistent enough for me to describe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I do notice. It’s not like I’m falling asleep at my desk all the time, exactly, but by the afternoon I feel really worn down. Like my body is technically there, but everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort.\n\nAt work, even something that should be small—like fixing an error in a spreadsheet, or finishing expense paperwork—can feel like I’m trying to push through mud with my brain. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are better days here and there, or maybe a week where things feel a little less heavy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a real stretch where I felt significantly better, like back to how I used to feel.\n\nIt’s been pretty continuous since… I guess about two years and a couple months ago now. So if you mean totally unable to carry out daily activities, then no, not in that extreme way.\n\nBut it has definitely affected how well I’m functioning. My performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of hard to read because that didn’t used to be something people said about me. It’s like I start things or I know what needs to happen, but then I don’t carry it all the way through consistently.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been dropping things I used to stay on top of. Soccer commitments, volunteering stuff, even planning for an MBA, which I used to be pretty organized about. I wouldn’t say I can’t do anything, but I’m not keeping up with my life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low, or is this the first time you've experienced this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had rough periods before, like stressful semesters or times where I felt down about something, but I don’t know if I’d put them in the same category as this.\n\nThis feels more sustained, I guess. Before, if I was low, I could usually point to a reason and it would shift after a while. Or I’d bounce back enough that it didn’t feel like it became the background of everything. With this, it’s harder to separate from my normal day-to-day now.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether I’ve had another period in my life that was like this, lasting and this low, I don’t think so. But I’m also second-guessing myself a little, because I don’t know where the line is between “normal hard time” and what you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low before this two-year period?\n\nPatient: No… not like this. I’ve been upset before, or stressed, or had times where I felt low for a bit, but this is different.\n\nThis is more like I’ve been walking around for a long time with this flat, heavy feeling that just sort of sits there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not literally every single second, I guess, but it feels like it’s there most of the time. Like even if I’m distracted or something is going okay, there’s still this flat, heavy feeling underneath it.

There are moments where it lifts a little, or maybe I don’t notice it as much—like if I’m busy at work or someone is being nice to me—but it doesn’t really feel like it fully goes away. It’s more like it fades into the background and then comes forward again. Most days I still feel down, kind of empty, like things don’t really reach me the way they should.

That’s honestly a big part of why I came in. It’s been going on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad mood that comes and goes anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I’ve felt… down, for a really long time. It’s hard to explain because things can be going fine on the outside — like work might be okay, or someone might be nice to me, or nothing specifically bad is happening — but I still feel kind of empty underneath it.\n\nIt’s like my mood just doesn’t really lift the way I think it should. I keep going through my day, doing what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this constant low feeling in the background. After a while it started to feel less like “I’m having a bad mood” and more like this is just how I am now, and that’s what made me think I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them in the same way or if I’m just doing them because they’re familiar, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “Oh, I suddenly stopped liking that.” It’s more blurry than that. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of something that should be relaxing or fun, and I’ll catch myself kind of checking whether I’m having a good time, which probably makes it harder to actually enjoy it. So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.\n\nLike if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. It’s not very clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure from activities or things that are typically enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I can still recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, and sometimes I do get moments where I think, okay, this is nice, or this is better than just sitting around. But it’s not like this strong feeling of pleasure, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it feels kind of muted. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some obvious, specific sleep problem that I can point to and say, “yes, this is definitely it.”\n\nI mean, I have normal nights where I sleep fine, and then nights where I’m kind of restless or I wake up and feel like I didn’t get enough, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just normal life stuff. I haven’t been tracking it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say with confidence.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a major sleep issue, at least not one that feels clear or consistent enough for me to describe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I do notice. It’s not like I’m falling asleep at my desk all the time, exactly, but by the afternoon I feel really worn down. Like my body is technically there, but everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort.\n\nAt work, even something that should be small—like fixing an error in a spreadsheet, or finishing expense paperwork—can feel like I’m trying to push through mud with my brain. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are better days here and there, or maybe a week where things feel a little less heavy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a real stretch where I felt significantly better, like back to how I used to feel.\n\nIt’s been pretty continuous since… I guess about two years and a couple months ago now. It started around when that dashboard project at work put me in the spotlight more than I was used to. I remember that because it felt like this clear before-and-after kind of point. Since then, even if some days are more manageable, I don’t think there’s been a period where I’d honestly say I felt much better overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your symptoms were so severe that you couldn't carry out your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had a period where I was, like, completely unable to function day to day. I still go to work, I still get through the basics, I pay bills, I eat, I show up most of the time. So if you mean totally unable to carry out daily activities, then no, not in that extreme way.\n\nBut it has definitely affected how well I’m functioning. My performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of hard to read because that didn’t used to be something people said about me. It’s like I start things or I know what needs to happen, but then I don’t carry it all the way through consistently.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been dropping things I used to stay on top of. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I can have parts of a day where I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking about it, or something at work goes okay, or someone is nice to me and I can respond normally on the outside. But I wouldn’t say I have days where I wake up and feel completely like myself and the low mood is just gone.

It’s more like… for a long time now I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling underneath everything. Even when nothing is especially wrong, or things are technically fine, I still feel down and kind of empty most days. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because it doesn’t feel like it fully lifts. It just gets quieter sometimes, and then I notice it again.

I keep going through my day, doing what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this constant low feeling in the background. I mean… I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them in the same way or if I’m just doing them because they’re familiar, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “Oh, I suddenly stopped liking that.” It’s more blurry than that. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of something that should be relaxing or fun, and I’ll catch myself kind of checking whether I’m having a good time, which probably makes it harder to actually enjoy it. So I don’t know if it’s changed recently exactly, or if I’ve just gotten used to things feeling a little muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. It’s more like… I still know what I usually like, and I still go through the motions of doing those things sometimes, but I don’t always feel strongly pulled toward them.\n\nLike if someone asked me, “Do you want to do something fun?” I might not have much of an answer. But I also don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I’m just kind of dull about everything lately. Sometimes I’ll start something I used to like and think, okay, maybe this will help, and then I spend half the time wondering whether I’m actually enjoying it or whether I’m just trying to prove to myself that I can.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed things feel less satisfying, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. It’s not very clear-cut for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure from activities or things that are typically enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I can still recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, and sometimes I do get moments where I think, okay, this is nice, or this is better than just sitting around. But it’s not like this strong feeling of pleasure, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it feels kind of muted. Like I’ll be doing something that should be fun, and part of me is almost observing it from the outside, like, “Am I enjoying this? Is this what enjoying something feels like?” And then once I start thinking about it that much, it’s hard to tell what I’m actually feeling.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I feel nothing. It’s not completely blank all the time. But I also don’t feel like I get as much out of things as I imagine other people do, or as much as I think I’m supposed to. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some obvious, specific sleep problem that I can point to and say, “yes, this is definitely it.”\n\nI mean, I have normal nights where I sleep fine, and then nights where I’m kind of restless or I wake up and feel like I didn’t get enough, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just normal life stuff. I haven’t been tracking it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say with confidence.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a major sleep issue, at least not one that feels clear or consistent enough for me to describe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I do notice. It’s not like I’m falling asleep at my desk all the time, exactly, but by the afternoon I feel really worn down. Like my body is technically there, but everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort.\n\nAt work, even something that should be small—like fixing an error in a spreadsheet, or finishing expense paperwork—can feel like I’m trying to push through mud with my brain. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are better days here and there, or maybe a week where things feel a little less heavy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a real stretch where I felt significantly better, like back to how I used to feel.\n\nIt’s been pretty continuous since… I guess about two years and a couple months ago now. It started around when that dashboard project at work put me in the spotlight more than I was used to. I remember that because it felt like this clear before-and-after kind of point. Since then, even if some days are more manageable, I don’t think there’s been a period where I’d honestly say I felt much better overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your symptoms were so severe that you couldn't carry out your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had a period where I was, like, completely unable to function day to day. I still go to work, I still get through the basics, I pay bills, I eat, I show up most of the time. So if you mean totally unable to carry out daily activities, then no, not in that extreme way.\n\nBut it has definitely affected how well I’m functioning. My performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of hard to read because that didn’t used to be something people said about me. It’s like I start things or I know what needs to happen, but then I don’t carry it all the way through consistently.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been dropping things I used to stay on top of. Soccer commitments, volunteering stuff, even planning for an MBA, which I used to be pretty organized about. I wouldn’t say I can’t do anything, but I’m not keeping up with my life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low, or is this the first time you've experienced this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. It’s more like… small flashes of enjoyment, maybe, but not something that really lasts or lifts my mood very much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if “sad” is always the exact word, because sometimes it’s less like crying-sad and more like this flat, heavy feeling I’m carrying around. But it’s there most days, honestly.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. For a long time now, even when nothing is specifically going wrong—like work might be fine, someone might be perfectly nice to me, I might have no real reason in that moment to feel bad—I still feel down. Kind of empty, I guess. Like there’s this weight in the background that doesn’t really match what’s happening around me.\n\nAnd I think that’s what makes it confusing, because I’ll look at my day and think, okay, nothing terrible happened, so why do I still feel like this? But I do. It’s just… there, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and two months, if I’m trying to be specific.\n\nI kind of trace it back to this dashboard project at work. I’m a management analyst, and that project unexpectedly put me more in the spotlight than I was used to. It wasn’t like one single dramatic day where everything changed, but around then is when I started noticing this more constant down feeling. At first I thought it was just stress from the project or from being watched more closely, like maybe once things settled down I’d go back to normal.\n\nBut it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed in the background after that, and then over time it became hard to tell where the project stress ended and where this general feeling began. So… yeah, roughly two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, but I haven’t had some big change where my clothes suddenly fit differently or people commented on it.\n\nI also haven’t been tracking it super closely, so I don’t want to sound too certain in a scientific way. But just from what I can tell day to day, nothing major has stood out to me over the past couple years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some obvious, specific sleep problem that I can point to and say, “yes, this is definitely it.”\n\nI mean, I have normal nights where I sleep fine, and then nights where I’m kind of restless or I wake up and feel like I didn’t get enough, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or just normal life stuff. I haven’t been tracking it or anything, so it’s hard for me to say with confidence.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a major sleep issue, at least not one that feels clear or consistent enough for me to describe.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I do notice. It’s not like I’m falling asleep at my desk all the time, exactly, but by the afternoon I feel really worn down. Like my body is technically there, but everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort.\n\nAt work, even something that should be small—like fixing an error in a spreadsheet, or finishing expense paperwork—can feel like I’m trying to push through mud with my brain. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are better days here and there, or maybe a week where things feel a little less heavy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a real stretch where I felt significantly better, like back to how I used to feel.\n\nIt’s been pretty continuous since… I guess about two years and a couple months ago now. It started around when that dashboard project at work put me in the spotlight more than I was used to. I remember that because it felt like this clear before-and-after kind of point. Since then, even if some days are more manageable, I don’t think there’s been a period where I’d honestly say I felt much better overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your symptoms were so severe that you couldn't carry out your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had a period where I was, like, completely unable to function day to day. I still go to work, I still get through the basics, I pay bills, I eat, I show up most of the time. So if you mean totally unable to carry out daily activities, then no, not in that extreme way.\n\nBut it has definitely affected how well I’m functioning. My performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of hard to read because that didn’t used to be something people said about me. It’s like I start things or I know what needs to happen, but then I don’t carry it all the way through consistently.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been dropping things I used to stay on top of. Soccer commitments, volunteering stuff, even planning for an MBA, which I used to be pretty organized about. I wouldn’t say I can’t do anything, but I’m not keeping up with my life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low, or is this the first time you've experienced this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had rough periods before, like stressful semesters or times where I felt down about something, but I don’t know if I’d put them in the same category as this.\n\nThis feels more sustained, I guess. Before, if I was low, I could usually point to a reason and it would shift after a while. Or I’d bounce back enough that it didn’t feel like it became the background of everything. Even when work is going okay, or someone is nice to me, or nothing especially bad is happening that day, I still feel down underneath it. Kind of empty, I guess. Like things don’t really land the way they should.\n\nSo before this two-year stretch, I don’t think I had felt this low in the same ongoing way. There were bad days before, definitely, but not this… not most days feeling like I’m carrying something heavy around.\n\nDoctor: Is your low mood something that you feel all the time, or are there times when it lifts and then comes back?\n\nPatient: It’s not literally every single second, I guess, but it feels like it’s there most of the time. Like even if I’m distracted or something is going okay, there’s still this flat, heavy feeling underneath it.\n\nThere are moments where it lifts a little, or maybe I don’t notice it as much—like if I’m busy at work or someone is being nice to me—but it doesn’t really feel like it fully goes away. It’s more like it fades into the background and then comes forward again. No… I don’t think so. Not for several weeks. I wish I could say there were stretches like that, where I felt like I was fully back to normal and the heaviness was just gone, but that’s not really how it’s been.

There are times when it’s less in my face, I guess. I’ll look at the thing and know it’s not complicated, but getting myself to actually do it feels exhausting.\n\nAnd it happens outside work too. Like getting myself to soccer, which should be something I just go do, can feel like this huge production. Not because I don’t know how or because it’s physically impossible, but because I feel so wiped out that just changing clothes and getting there feels like dragging myself along. So… yes, I’d say I’m lacking energy pretty often, especially later in the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are better days here and there, or maybe a week where things feel a little less heavy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a real stretch where I felt significantly better, like back to how I used to feel.\n\nIt’s been pretty continuous since… I guess about two years and a couple months ago now. It started around when that dashboard project at work put me in the spotlight more than I was used to. I remember that because it felt like this clear before-and-after kind of point. Since then, even if some days are more manageable, I don’t think there’s been a period where I’d honestly say I felt much better overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your symptoms were so severe that you couldn't carry out your daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had a period where I was, like, completely unable to function day to day. I still go to work, I still get through the basics, I pay bills, I eat, I show up most of the time. So if you mean totally unable to carry out daily activities, then no, not in that extreme way.\n\nBut it has definitely affected how well I’m functioning. My performance review actually mentioned that I’m missing follow-through now, which was kind of hard to read because that didn’t used to be something people said about me. It’s like I start things or I know what needs to happen, but then I don’t carry it all the way through consistently.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been dropping things I used to stay on top of. Soccer commitments, volunteering stuff, even planning for an MBA, which I used to be pretty organized about. I wouldn’t say I can’t do anything, but I’m not keeping up with my life the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low, or is this the first time you've experienced this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had rough periods before, like stressful semesters or times where I felt down about something, but I don’t know if I’d put them in the same category as this.\n\nThis feels more sustained, I guess. Before, if I was low, I could usually point to a reason and it would shift after a while. Or I’d bounce back enough that it didn’t feel like it became the background of everything. With this, it’s harder to separate from my normal day-to-day now.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether I’ve had another period in my life that was like this, lasting and this low, I don’t think so. But I’m also second-guessing myself a little, because I don’t know where the line is between “normal hard time” and what you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low before this two-year period?\n\nPatient: No… not like this. I’ve been upset before, or stressed, or had times where I felt low for a bit, but this is different.\n\nThis is more like I’ve been walking around for a long time with this flat, heavy feeling that just sort of sits there. Even when work is going okay, or someone is nice to me, or nothing especially bad is happening that day, I still feel down underneath it. Kind of empty, I guess. Like things don’t really land the way they should.\n\nSo before this two-year stretch, I don’t think I had felt this low in the same ongoing way. There were bad days before, definitely, but not this… not most days feeling like I’m carrying something heavy around.\n\nDoctor: Is your low mood something that you feel all the time, or are there times when it lifts and then comes back?\n\nPatient: It’s not literally every single second, I guess, but it feels like it’s there most of the time. Even when nothing is especially wrong, or things are technically fine, I still feel down and kind of empty most days. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because it doesn’t feel like it fully lifts. It just gets quieter sometimes, and then I notice it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you feel completely back to your normal self, without this low mood, that last for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not for several weeks. I wish I could say there were stretches like that, where I felt like I was fully back to normal and the heaviness was just gone, but that’s not really how it’s been.\n\nThere are times when it’s less in my face, I guess. Like if work is going okay, or I’m busy enough that I’m just moving through tasks, or someone is being nice to me and I can kind of respond and seem normal. But underneath that, it’s still there. That flat, heavy feeling. I still feel down and kind of empty most days, even when there isn’t some obvious reason for it.\n\nThat’s actually a big reason I came in. It’s been going on long enough that I can’t really point to a few weeks where I felt completely like myself again. It’s more like it dulls down sometimes, but it doesn’t really leave.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have distinct episodes of feeling low, or is it more of a continuous feeling?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / management_analyst / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"I came in because for a long time now I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling, like even when work is going okay or people are nice to me, I still feel down and kind of empty most days.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more continuous, honestly. I mean, I wouldn’t describe it like these clear separate episodes where I’m okay for a while and then suddenly I drop into feeling low. It’s more like this baseline I’ve been carrying around for a long time.

That’s kind of why I came in, because I’ve been walking around with this flat, heavy feeling most of the time. Even when work is going okay, or someone’s being nice to me, or nothing is technically wrong in that moment, I still feel down and kind of empty underneath it. Sometimes I’m distracted enough that I’m not focused on it, but it doesn’t feel like it really goes away. It’s just… there, most days.
